Thursday, May 16, 2024 Austin City Council Regular Meeting
Official City Record Agenda Watch Video Transcript

  • Major Housing Density Debate (HOME Phase 2):

    The Council heard extensive public testimony on proposals to reduce minimum lot sizes, allowing more housing units on single-family properties to address Austin's housing crisis.

  • Affordability vs. Displacement:

    Supporters argued these changes would increase housing supply and affordability, particularly for workers and multi-generational families. Opponents warned of accelerated gentrification, displacement of current residents, and a rise in luxury housing.

  • Call for Anti-Displacement Safeguards:

    Many community groups and residents urged the Council to delay the vote and implement an "equity and anti-displacement overlay" to protect vulnerable neighborhoods and ensure truly affordable housing outcomes.

  • Infrastructure and Environmental Concerns:

    Significant worries were voiced regarding the impact of increased density on city infrastructure, including worsened flooding, traffic congestion, strain on utilities, and critical tree loss.

So while I understand and appreciate the intent to increase affordability in Austin, these changes drastically upend long standing development regulations at what seems like the request of a few over the objection of many we heard from those individuals yesterday. Unfortunately, while some cities have made similar changes , there simply hasn't been enough time for meaningful [1:26:34 PM] enough time for meaningful results to be measured, leaving us conducting what may seem to many like a nother social experiment without clear evidence of success and the potential of unintended consequences for this proposal looming for our present and future residents. Compatibility standards, for example, are in place for the enjoyment of our residential properties, especially from a privacy perspective. In fact, just the other day, I was talking to a neighbor who shared their experience about how current compatibility standards allowed them to enjoy their backyard without the looming presence of towering developments invading their privacy. We heard testimony that tall buildings near single family homes can cause light pollution for neighbors. These changes threaten to increase the proliferation of strs tree removal, impervious cover, the urban heat island effect strain on our electrical grid and water supply, and undermine flood prevention measures. These are all critical to maintaining safety and the quality of life we cherish and the environment we strive to protect. Proponents of these changes, particularly the itod, are clear that upzoning is intended to support mass transit , but I believe mass transit should be created to support our residents way of life, not the other way around. I'm not supportive of any attempt to reengineer our city's housing to support any mass transit system, let alone one that is fraught with cost overruns, legal problems, and has been drastically reduced in scope compared to what voters were [1:28:36 PM] compared to what voters were promised in 2020. In terms of affordability, I believe these changes will create upward pressure on property tax valuations and everyday austinites property tax bills. Property owners and renters alike will feel that economic pressure to move out of and sell existing housing stock, concentrating much of our city's property wealth into the hands of large corporate investors to subsidize and redevelop into higher density residential. I've seen no convincing evidence that these newly developed smaller lots will be any more affordable to the average austinite, especially compared to the quality of life that will be sacrificed in exchange for.
